Ground State Energy Fluctuations of Pinned Elastic Manifolds
Authors: Yan V. Fyodorov, Bertrand Lacroix-A-Chez-Toine, Pierre Le Doussal
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We describe the atypical fluctuations of the ground state energy of the random elastic manifold, a disordered model defined on a lattice of linear size L with internal dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le d<4$$\end{document}0≤d<4 embedded in a medium of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\gg 1$$\end{document}N≫1. The ground-state energy results from a competition between confinement, elasticity and disorder. We obtain an exact description of the large deviation rate function with speed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NL^d$$\end{document}NLd and its different phases, corresponding to different patterns of replica symmetry breaking (RSB). Our results show that the ground-state energy satisfies a central limit theorem and we obtain an explicit expression for the rescaled variance. In the (massless) limit of zero confinement, this variance vanishes for short-range disorder and the ground-state energy displays super-concentration. From our results on the large deviation function, we characterise explicitly the left tail of the distribution of the typical fluctuations of the ground state energy. It displays an exponential tail for a one step RSB pattern while for a full RSB pattern it decays super-exponentially with a non trivial exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ that we compute explicitly.

Introduction

The optimal configuration adopted at equilibrium by many physical systems results from the competition between elasticity, promoting flat, homogeneous configurations, and disorder, promoting rugged, heterogeneous configurations [1]. The elastic manifold is a versatile and well-studied model to describe such physical systems. Let us define it on a d-dimensional lattice with linear size L and a lattice spacing a. At each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}$$\end{document}x of the lattice we define a N-dimensional field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{u}(\textbf{x})$$\end{document}u(x). The equilibrium static properties of this Hamiltonian are studied at finite inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} converges towards the average ground-state energy6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} in the low temperature regime: (i) the functional renormalisation group, valid for any N but perturbative around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=4$$\end{document}d=4 [2], and (ii) the replica saddle point method in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞ which is valid for any d [3]. The matching between these quite different approaches at large but finite N was investigated in [4–7]. The focus of the present article is to compute the fluctuations of the ground state energy in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}N→∞ using approach (ii). It is worth noting that the variance of the difference in ground state energy induced by a small perturbing field was computed in that limit in [7], with applications to avalanches and shocks in the closely related problem of Burger’s turbulence [8–10]. However, to our knowledge, neither the variance of the ground state energy, nor its large deviation function, have been explicitly computed previously.

In that limit, the rescaled free-energy is a self-averaging random variables, i.e.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\mathcal{F}_{N,L}(\beta )=\lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\frac{F_{N,L}(\beta )}{NL^d}\underset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\left\langle \mathcal{F}_{N,L}(\beta ) \right\rangle =\mathcal{F}_{d}(\beta ,L)\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→∞FN,L(β)=limN→∞FN,L(β)NLd=a.s.limN→∞FN,L(β)=Fd(β,L),and the limiting quenched free energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{F}_{d}(\beta ,L)$$\end{document}Fd(β,L) has been computed explicitly in the physics literature using replica and saddle-point techniques, notably by Mezard and Parisi [3] (see also [11–13]). These results have recently been re-examined using rigorous methods in a series of papers by Ben Arous and Kivimae [14, 15]. The limiting free energy is then found to be represented via the "Parisi formula" as the solution of an optimisation problem over a space of probability measures, with the optimal probability measure describing the overlap distribution between different configurations optimising the free-energy. The self-averaging property holds similarly at zero temperature and a Parisi formula can similarly be derived for the limiting ground-state energy, which concentrates around the typical value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{L,\mathrm typ}(\mu )=\lim _{N\rightarrow \infty }\mathcal{E}_{N,L}/(NL^d)=\lim _{\beta \rightarrow \infty }\mathcal{F}_{d}(\beta ,L)$$\end{document}eL,typ(μ)=limN→∞EN,L/(NLd)=limβ→∞Fd(β,L).

A particularly interesting outcome of the paper by Mezard and Parisi [3] is the computation of the scaling behaviour of the variance of the free energy with the size L of the lattice. Using dimensional analysis and renormalisation group arguments, they showed that in the massless limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0 a simple relation exists between the growth exponent of the free energy variance with the lattice size in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} correlation functions f(q) as defined in Eq. The variance can be shown to diverge in the continuum limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L/a\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}L/a→∞ in any dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\ge 4$$\end{document}d≥4. Interestingly, it was found that the exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi > 0$$\end{document}χ>0 is positive when the free-energy is given by its full replica-symmetry broken (FRSB) expression while it is negative or zero otherwise. For negative exponents, the variance of the free-energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{N,L}(\beta )$$\end{document}FN,L(β) decreases with the lattice size L and the free-energy is thus a self-averaging variable in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}L→∞. The rescaled free-energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{F}_{N,L}(\beta )$$\end{document}FN,L(β) is instead self-averaging in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}L→∞ whenever the weaker criterion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta < 1$$\end{document}ζ<1 is satisfied.

In this case the fluctuations of the ground state energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin can be investigated directly.

(ii) Long range disorder, such that the disordered potential is a random process with stationary increments, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(q) - f(0) =\int _0^{\infty } dk\,e^{-q k^2}\,\tilde{f}(k)$$\end{document}f(q)-f(0)=∫0∞dke-qk2f~(k) with positive \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{f}(k)\ge 0$$\end{document}f~(k)≥0 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 0$$\end{document}k≥0. In this case, the fluctuations of the energy difference with respect to the flat reference configuration need to be considered14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Delta e_{\min }= \frac{1}{NL^d}\min _{\textbf{u}(\textbf{x})} ( \mathcal{H}[\textbf{u}] - \mathcal{H}[\textbf{0}] ) = \frac{1}{NL^d}\left( \mathcal{E}_{N,L} - \sum _\textbf{x} V(\textbf{0},\textbf{x})\right) \;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Δemin=1NLdminu(x)(H[u]-H[0])=1NLdEN,L-∑xV(0,x).The random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta e_{\min }$$\end{document}Δemin is by definition a negative random variable and the probability that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta e_{\min }>0$$\end{document}Δemin>0 is identically zero. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(\textbf{0},\textbf{x})$$\end{document}V(0,x) is a zero average Gaussian random variable, it is simple to check that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta e_{\min }$$\end{document}Δemin have the same average value. Finally, let us mention that this average value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle e_{\min } \right\rangle =\left\langle \Delta e_{\min } \right\rangle $$\end{document}emin=Δemin depends on the microscopic details, in particular the lattice sizes a and L and it can be shown to diverge for any internal dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2 in the continuum limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L/a\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}L/a→∞.

As has been shown both in the physics [3] and mathematics [14, 15] literature, the typical value of the ground-state energy is determined by the replica symmetric expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ above the so-called Larkin mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{c}$$\end{document}μc. The latter is defined as the unique solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f''(0)I_2(\mu =\mu _{c})=1$$\end{document}f′′(0)I2(μ=μc)=1, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_2(\mu )$$\end{document}I2(μ) defined by taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=2$$\end{document}p=2 in the expression15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_p(x)=\int _\textbf{k}\frac{1}{\left( x-\mu +\nu (\textbf{k})\right) ^p}\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ip(x)=∫k1x-μ+ν(k)p.This Larkin mass has been recently shown not only to determine the onset of the replica-symmetry breaking transition but also the associated topology trivialisation transition from an energy function (Hamiltonian) with a single minimum (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \ge \mu _c$$\end{document}μ≥μc) to a Hamiltonian displaying a number of minima growing exponentially with the dimension N (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu <\mu _c$$\end{document}μ<μc) [18, 19]. We will show in the following that the Larkin mass also plays an important role in the description of higher-order cumulants of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin.

In the following, we will focus our attention on the cumulants of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin and its large deviation function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L(e,\mu )$$\end{document}ΛL(e,μ) for short-range disorder and provide some results for the cumulants of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta e_{\min }$$\end{document}Δemin for long-range disorder.

The paper is structured as follows. In section 2 we summarise the main results of the paper, providing a Parisi formula for the cumulant generating function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin as well as explicit expressions for the rescaled variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{V}_L(\mu )$$\end{document}VL(μ) and the large deviation function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _L(e,\mu )$$\end{document}ΛL(e,μ). In section 3 we provide the detailed derivation of the expression of the cumulant generating function as an optimisation problem. In section 4 we analyse the expression of the cumulant generating function and large deviation function at different levels of replica-symmetry breaking. In section 5 we obtain explicit expressions for the large deviation function in the continuous limit in a few specific internal dimensions d and for some choice of covariance function f(q). Finally in section 6 we conclude and provide some ideas for future directions. Some technical details are relegated to the appendices. In appendix A we analyse the average of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin. In appendix B we compute the annealed complexity of minima at fixed energy which provides a bound for the large deviation function.

Summary of the Main Results

Unless specified otherwise, the results below apply for the ground-state energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin for a short-range disorder such that the correlation function decays at infinite distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{q\rightarrow \infty }f(q)=0$$\end{document}limq→∞f(q)=0. The results for the ground-state energy difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta e_{\min }$$\end{document}Δemin for a long-range disorder are summarised in the last subsection.

Typical Fluctuations

For any value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >0$$\end{document}μ>0, we obtain an explicit expression for the rescaled variance16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal{V}_{L}(\mu )=&\lim _{N \rightarrow \infty }\frac{\textrm{Var}(\mathcal{E}_{N,L})}{N L^d}=f\left( Q_\textrm{typ}\right) -\frac{I_2(\mu ) f'\left( Q_\textrm{typ}\right) ^2}{2}\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VL(μ)=limN→∞Var(EN,L)NLd=fQtyp-I2(μ)f′Qtyp22,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}Qtyp is the difference between largest and smallest overlap values, i.e. the range of the support of "Parisi function" describing the typical distribution of overlaps. In the RS phase, the distribution of overlaps is supported on a single atom and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}=0$$\end{document}Qtyp=0 while in the replica symmetry broken phases (both 1RSB and FRSB) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}>0$$\end{document}Qtyp>0. The expression for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}Qtyp is RS, hence vanishes, for any mass larger than the Larkin mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >\mu _c$$\end{document}μ>μc and RSB for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu <\mu _c$$\end{document}μ<μc [3, 14]. The criterion for selecting the pattern of replica symmetry breaking is not simple but still explicit (see Eq. (A32) and the following paragraph).

In the FRSB phase, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}Qtyp is given by a simple expression17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q_\textrm{typ}={f''}^{-1}\left( \frac{1}{I_2(\mu )}\right) \;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Qtyp=f′′-11I2(μ),where we remind that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_p(\mu )$$\end{document}Ip(μ) is defined from Eq. (15) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f''}^{-1}$$\end{document}f′′-1 is the inverse function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f''$$\end{document}f′′. In the 1RSB phase, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}Qtyp is the positive solution of Eqs. (95-96).

This rescaled variance is strictly positive for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >0$$\end{document}μ>0 and as a direct consequence, the ground-state energy satisfies the central limit theorem18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\textrm{Prob}\left[ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle }{\sqrt{\textrm{Var}(\mathcal{E}_{N,L})}}\le x\right] =\frac{1}{2}\left( 1+\textrm{erf}\left( \frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}\right) \right) \;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limNLd→∞ProbEN,L-EN,LVar(EN,L)≤x=121+erfx2.The energy fluctuations are thus of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim (NL^d)^{1/2}$$\end{document}∼(NLd)1/2.

As we show below, the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}Qtyp diverges in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0 in any internal dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<d<4$$\end{document}0<d<4 which implies that the rescaled variance in Eq. (16) vanishes. This in turn implies that the ground-state energy density "super-concentrates", similarly to what is observed for spherical spin-models at zero magnetic field [20], and the central limit theorem breaks down in that limit. Our results indicate that in the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0 the limiting distribution takes a different form for different patterns of replica symmetry breaking. The energy fluctuations are thus of order O(1). This exponent can be recovered by a simple matching argument between the tail of the regime of typical fluctuations described by Eq. (22) and the large deviation regime in Eq. (23), expressing both the rescaled variables x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle $$\end{document}EN,L-EN,L and matching the powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NL^d$$\end{document}NLd in both equations.The behaviour described above is reminiscent of what has been observed for the fluctuations of the spherical spin model in [21]. It was shown there that under the FRSB ansatz the ground-state fluctuations display a non-trivial exponent while such an exponent is always equal to unity within the 1RSB ansatz. Using an explicit approach to derive the large deviation function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}(\epsilon )$$\end{document}L(ϵ) in the following sections, we determine independently the FRSB value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ in Eq. (22) and of the exponential tail (i.e. an exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =1$$\end{document}ξ=1) in the 1RSB pattern in Eq. (20). Let us now provide more details on our results for the cumulant generating function and large deviation function of the ground-state energy.

For a 1RSB pattern, we conjecture the limiting distribution 19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\textrm{Prob}\left[ \mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle \le x\right] =\mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm 1RSB}(x)\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limNLd→∞ProbEN,L-EN,L≤x=Pd,1RSB(x). The energy fluctuations are thus of order O(1). This limiting distribution displays an exponential left tail 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<\Sigma _{\min }'(e_\textrm{typ},0)=\lim _{x\rightarrow -\infty }\frac{1}{x}\ln \mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm 1RSB}(x)<\infty \;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<Σmin′(etyp,0)=limx→-∞1xlnPd,1RSB(x)<∞, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\min }(e,\mu )$$\end{document}Σmin(e,μ) is the annealed complexity of minima at energy density e and for mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_\textrm{typ}=\lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\mathcal{E}_{N,L}/(NL^d)$$\end{document}etyp=limNLd→∞EN,L/(NLd).

For a 1RSB pattern, we conjecture the limiting distribution 19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\textrm{Prob}\left[ \mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle \le x\right] =\mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm 1RSB}(x)\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limNLd→∞ProbEN,L-EN,L≤x=Pd,1RSB(x). The energy fluctuations are thus of order O(1). This limiting distribution displays an exponential left tail 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<\Sigma _{\min }'(e_\textrm{typ},0)=\lim _{x\rightarrow -\infty }\frac{1}{x}\ln \mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm 1RSB}(x)<\infty \;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<Σmin′(etyp,0)=limx→-∞1xlnPd,1RSB(x)<∞, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\min }(e,\mu )$$\end{document}Σmin(e,μ) is the annealed complexity of minima at energy density e and for mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_\textrm{typ}=\lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\mathcal{E}_{N,L}/(NL^d)$$\end{document}etyp=limNLd→∞EN,L/(NLd).

In contrast, when the solution to Parisi optimization problem is controlled by FRSB pattern, we conjecture the following limiting distribution of typical fluctuations 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\textrm{Prob}\left[ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle }{(NL^d)^{\chi /d}}\le x\right] =\mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm FRSB}(x)\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limNLd→∞ProbEN,L-EN,L(NLd)χ/d≤x=Pd,FRSB(x), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \chi <2$$\end{document}0≤χ<2 is the exponent of the variance computed by Mezard and Parisi (which depends on the asymptotic behaviour of the covariance function f(q)). The energy fluctuations in this case are thus of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim (NL^d)^{\chi /d}$$\end{document}∼(NLd)χ/d with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\chi /d<1/2$$\end{document}0<χ/d<1/2. Additionally, we conjecture that the limiting distribution displays the left tail behaviour 22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\ln \mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm FRSB}(x)\approx r_d(-x)^{\xi }\;,\;\;x\rightarrow -\infty \;,\;\;\textrm{with}\;\;\xi =\frac{d}{d-\chi }\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-lnPd,FRSB(x)≈rd(-x)ξ,x→-∞,withξ=dd-χ, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_d$$\end{document}rd is a constant that can be computed explicitly. The exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ corresponds to that of the large deviation function in the vicinity of the typical value, i.e. 23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}(\epsilon )=-\lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\frac{1}{NL^d}\ln \textrm{Prob}\left[ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle }{NL^d}\le \epsilon \right] =\lim _{L\rightarrow \infty }\Lambda _L(e_{L,\mathrm typ}(0)+\epsilon ,0)\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(ϵ)=-limNLd→∞1NLdlnProbEN,L-EN,LNLd≤ϵ=limL→∞ΛL(eL,typ(0)+ϵ,0), where the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}(\epsilon )\approx r_d(-\epsilon )^{\xi }$$\end{document}L(ϵ)≈rd(-ϵ)ξ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow 0_-$$\end{document}ϵ→0-. This exponent can be recovered by a simple matching argument between the tail of the regime of typical fluctuations described by Eq. (22) and the large deviation regime in Eq. (23), expressing both the rescaled variables x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle $$\end{document}EN,L-EN,L and matching the powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NL^d$$\end{document}NLd in both equations.

In contrast, when the solution to Parisi optimization problem is controlled by FRSB pattern, we conjecture the following limiting distribution of typical fluctuations 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{NL^d\rightarrow \infty }\textrm{Prob}\left[ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle }{(NL^d)^{\chi /d}}\le x\right] =\mathcal{P}_{d,\mathrm FRSB}(x)\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limNLd→∞ProbEN,L-EN,L(NLd)χ/d≤x=Pd,FRSB(x), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \chi <2$$\end{document}0≤χ<2 is the exponent of the variance computed by Mezard and Parisi (which depends on the asymptotic behaviour of the covariance function f(q)). This exponent can be recovered by a simple matching argument between the tail of the regime of typical fluctuations described by Eq. (22) and the large deviation regime in Eq. (23), expressing both the rescaled variables x and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{E}_{N,L}-\left\langle \mathcal{E}_{N,L} \right\rangle $$\end{document}EN,L-EN,L and matching the powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$NL^d$$\end{document}NLd in both equations.

General Expression for the Cumulant Generating Function

The scaled cumulant generating function (CGF) of the ground-state energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin is expressed as24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} _{g(\textbf{k},z),G_0(\textbf{k}),v(\textbf{k}),w_M}\mathcal{S}_{0,L}(\mu )& \;,\;\;s<0\;, \\ 0& \;,\;\;s=0\;, \\ \displaystyle \min _{g(\textbf{k},z),G_0(\textbf{k}),v(\textbf{k}),w_M}\mathcal{S}_{0,L}(\mu )& \;,\;\;s>0\;, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}φL(s,μ)=limN→∞1NLdlne-sNLdemin=maxg(k,z),G0(k),v(k),wMS0,L(μ),s<0,0,s=0,ming(k,z),G0(k),v(k),wMS0,L(μ),s>0,where the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{S}_{0,L}(\mu )$$\end{document}S0,L(μ) reads25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_M$$\end{document}wM satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Optimising over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_M$$\end{document}wM yields the explicit boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(\textbf{k},z\ge w_M)=0$$\end{document}g(k,z≥wM)=0. The expression above is valid for any pattern of replica-symmetry breaking (replica-symmetric (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_M=s$$\end{document}wM=s), one-step replica symmetry breaking, full replica-symmetry breaking).

Remark: For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\ne 0$$\end{document}s≠0, the expression of the cumulant generating function results from the properties of optimal atypical configurations. The values of the different parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_M,v(\textbf{k}),G_0(\textbf{k}),g(\textbf{k},z)$$\end{document}wM,v(k),G0(k),g(k,z) in the optimisation problem above describe the statistical properties of these optimal atypical configurations (see section 3.4 for a detailed discussion).

As we show in appendix B, using an exact result relying on the Kac-Rice approach, the cumulant generating function is bounded from above as27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varphi _L(s,\mu )\le \frac{s^2 f(0)}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int _\textbf{k}\ln \left( 1-\frac{s f'(0)}{\nu (\textbf{k})}\right) +\Sigma _{\min }(\mu -s f'(0))\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}φL(s,μ)≤s2f(0)2+12∫kln1-sf′(0)ν(k)+Σmin(μ-sf′(0)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\min }(\mu )$$\end{document}Σmin(μ) is the annealed complexity of energy functional minima for the mass parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu -s f'(1)>\mu _c$$\end{document}μ-sf′(1)>μc one finds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma _{\min }(\mu -s f'(1))=0$$\end{document}Σmin(μ-sf′(1))=0 and the term on the right-hand side is shown to coincide with the replica-symmetric (RS) expression of the cumulant generating function, corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_M=s$$\end{document}wM=s.

(28) corresponds to the limit of zero temperature of the free-energy derived using the replica method in [3] and has recently been obtained rigorously in [15] (although for the latter the equivalence between the formulae is much less obvious).

The expression for the cumulant generating function obtained above allows to derive higher-order cumulants and, in particular, the variance of the rescaled ground state energy. The latter is defined as30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal{V}_{L}(\mu )=&\lim _{N \rightarrow \infty }\frac{\textrm{Var}(\mathcal{E}_{N,L})}{N L^d}=f\left( Q_\textrm{typ}\right) -\frac{I_2(\mu ) f'\left( Q_\textrm{typ}\right) ^2}{2}\;,\;\; \textrm{where}\;\;Q_\textrm{typ}=\int _\textbf{k}g_\textrm{typ}(\textbf{k},0)\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}VL(μ)=limN→∞Var(EN,L)NLd=fQtyp-I2(μ)f′Qtyp22,whereQtyp=∫kgtyp(k,0).The integral \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_2(x)$$\end{document}I2(x) is defined in Eq. (15). The expression in Eq. In the simplest, replica-symmetric (RS) phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{typ}=0$$\end{document}Qtyp=0. The exponent for the variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi $$\end{document}χ is positive in the FRSB regime while the exponent for the rescaled variance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\chi }=\chi -d/2=2(1-\gamma )\zeta _\textrm{FRSB}$$\end{document}χ~=χ-d/2=2(1-γ)ζFRSB is negative.

The exponent in Eq. (43) is in full agreement with the results of Mezard and Parisi [3] using an approach relying on dimensional analysis and renormalisation group. Our approach relies instead on an explicit computation of the ground state energy variance and allows us to provide not only the value for the scaling exponent, but also a quantitative prediction for the associated scaling function. The criterion for the variance increasing with the system size, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi \ge 0$$\end{document}χ≥0, is equivalent to the condition of validity of the FRSB ansatz (either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<2$$\end{document}d<2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma < \gamma _c=2/(2-d) $$\end{document}γ<γc=2/(2-d)). Furthermore, using our results, one can compute the exact expression for the variance for any given correlation function f(q). We now provide detailed results on the large deviation function of the ground-state energy.

Ground State Energy Large Deviation Function for Short-Ranged Correlated Disorder

The LDF of the ground-state energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin is defined in Eq. (12) displays several branches reflecting different pattern of replica symmetry breaking. In this section, we provide explicit results for the expression of these branches for short-ranged correlation functions f(q).

Replica-Symmetric Branch

The expression of the large deviation function within the replica-symmetric (RS) ansatz can be obtained either by applying the replica method or using the lower bound obtained from applying the Kac-Rice method. The RS branch can be expressed parametrically as46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} (96). The quadratic behaviour of the LDF is consistent with the central limit theorem describing the typical fluctuations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_{\min }$$\end{document}emin. The asymptotic behaviour of the large deviation function as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}e→-∞ is universal and reads52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda _{L,\mathrm RS}(e,\mu )\approx \frac{e^2}{2f(0)}\;,\;\;e\rightarrow -\infty \;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΛL,RS(e,μ)≈e22f(0),e→-∞.Let us now consider the results obtained for branches with replica-symmetry breaking, starting with the case of one-step replica-symmetry breaking.

Finally, the asymptotic behaviour of the large deviation function as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}e→+∞ can be obtained and shown to be non universal, in contrast to the behaviour as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}e→-∞ (see Eq. A careful analysis shows that in this regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}Q→∞ with the scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=\mu /f'(Q)+O(1)$$\end{document}s=μ/f′(Q)+O(1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e=\mu Q/2+O(1)$$\end{document}e=μQ/2+O(1) while the parameter w reaches an asymptotic value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{\infty }=O(1)$$\end{document}w∞=O(1). Finally, the asymptotic behaviour of the large deviation function as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}e→+∞ can be explored. The asymptotic behaviour is exactly the same as for the 1RSB case in Eq. (68). Note that this behaviour is qualitatively very different from the large deviation function of spherical spin-glasses. For the latter, as conjectured in [21] and subsequently proved rigorously in [22], the large deviation function with speed N of the ground-state energy diverges at a finite value of energy indicating a transition towards a regime of large deviation with speed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^2$$\end{document}N2 (see also [23–25] for similar results for the SK model). The presence of the harmonic confinement seems to prevent the occurrence of such transition for the elastic manifold. Massless Limit and Superconcentration

FRSB Branch

To prevent divergence of the expressions, we will work in the following with the variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon =e-e_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}ϵ=e-etyp which remains of order O(1) in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0 in any dimension. From the definition of Q in that phase in Eq. 1RSB Branch

The behaviour of the large deviation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<2$$\end{document}d<2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \ge 2/(d-2)$$\end{document}γ≥2/(d-2) can be understood by analogy with the study of spherical spin glasses in [21]. It was found there that the full large deviation function is given at zero magnetic field by its RS branch while the 1RSB phase is limited to a point. One can check similarly here that the large deviation function undergoes a discontinuous transition at the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_\textrm{dis}\equiv e_\textrm{typ}$$\end{document}edis≡etyp from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q=0$$\end{document}Q=0 in the RS phase to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}Q→∞ in the 1RSB phase. Optimisation Problem

In this section, we derive the general expression of the cumulant generating function for the ground-state energy in terms of a functional optimisation problem.

Integer Moments

To compute the large deviation function, we first compute exactly the integer moments of the partition function. The integer moments of the centred free-energy read [13]97\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left\langle Z_{N,L,\eta }(\beta )^n \right\rangle \nonumber \\&=\int \prod _{a=1}^n \mathcal{D} \textbf{u}_a(\textbf{x})\,\left\langle e^{-\beta \sum _{a=1}^n \left( \mathcal{H}[\textbf{u}_a]-\eta \mathcal{H}[\textbf{0}]\right) } \right\rangle \nonumber \\ =&\int \prod _{a=1}^n \mathcal{D} \textbf{u}_a(\textbf{x})\,\exp \left[ \frac{N}{2}\left( \beta ^2\sum _\textbf{x}\sum _{a,b}\left[ f\left( \frac{(\textbf{u}_a(\textbf{x})-\textbf{u}_b(\textbf{x}))^2}{2N}\right) -2\eta f\left( \frac{\textbf{u}_a(\textbf{x})^2}{2N}\right) +\eta f(0)\right] \right. \right. \nonumber \\&\left. \left. -\frac{\beta }{2}\sum _{a}\sum _{\textbf{x},\textbf{y}} \textbf{u}_a(\textbf{x})(\mu \delta _{\textbf{x},\textbf{y}}-t \Delta _{\textbf{x},\textbf{y}})\textbf{u}_a(\textbf{y})\right) \right] \;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZN,L,η(β)n=∫∏a=1nDua(x)e-β∑a=1nH[ua]-ηH[0]=∫∏a=1nDua(x)expN2β2∑x∑a,bf(ua(x)-ub(x))22N-2ηfua(x)22N+ηf(0)-β2∑a∑x,yua(x)(μδx,y-tΔx,y)ua(y),where the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =0,1$$\end{document}η=0,1 allows to treat on the same footing short-range and long-range disorder. Zero-Temperature Limit

To obtain the expression of the cumulant generating function of the ground-state energy, we need to take the specific scaling limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}n→0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}β→∞ with fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=n\beta =O(1)$$\end{document}s=nβ=O(1). Interpretation of the Optimal Fluctuations

Let us briefly discuss the probabilistic meaning of these parameters in the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\ge 0$$\end{document}s≥0. To this end, we define the cumulative distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{\beta }(q)$$\end{document}xβ(q) of overlaps between replicas121\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_{ab}=\int _\textbf{k}\frac{\textbf{u}_a(\textbf{k})\cdot \textbf{u}_b(-\textbf{k})}{N}\;, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qab=∫kua(k)·ub(-k)N,at finite inverse temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. This function is a non-decreasing function of q with the boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{\beta }(0\le q\le q_0)=n$$\end{document}xβ(0≤q≤q0)=n, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_0$$\end{document}q0 the minimal overlap and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<n<1$$\end{document}0<n<1, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{\beta }(q\ge q_d)=1$$\end{document}xβ(q≥qd)=1 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_d=q_{aa}$$\end{document}qd=qaa the maximal (self)-overlap. The boundary condition can be interpreted as imposing an atom at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=0$$\end{document}q=0 with probability weight n. If the optimal solution corresponds to a finite number k of replica-symmetry breaking, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{\beta }(q)$$\end{document}xβ(q) displays a finite number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k+1$$\end{document}k+1 of steps. Analysis of the Different Phases

Let us now consider in more detail the expression of the cumulant generating function, large deviation function and variance in the different phase of replica-symmetry for short-range disorder.

Finally, let us consider the asymptotic limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}s→-∞. (209) remains finite. Thus, in the limit, one must have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q(s)\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}Q(s)→+∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}s→-∞. (213), one obtains that the asymptotic behaviour as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}s→-∞, i.e. as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}ϵ→+∞ reads221\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda _{L,\mathrm FRSB}(e,\mu )\approx \frac{\mu ^2 f\left( 2e/\mu \right) }{2f'\left( 2e/\mu \right) ^2}\;,\;\;e\rightarrow +\infty \;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΛL,FRSB(e,μ)≈μ2f2e/μ2f′2e/μ2,e→+∞.Thus for any positive \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >0$$\end{document}μ>0, comparing with the result obtained in the 1RSB pattern in Eq. (175), the asymptotic behaviour of the large deviation function as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}e→+∞ does not seem to depend on the RSB pattern or the dimension but depends explicitly on the correlation function of the disorder. For models displaying a FRSB pattern as well and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >0$$\end{document}μ>0, as the large deviation function is well-defined for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}e∈R, one does not expect a regime of large deviation with speed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^2$$\end{document}N2, in contrast to what has been observed for spherical spin-glasses [21, 22]. In the following section, we apply our general results to a few special cases and consider in particular some cases where the unconfined limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0 can be taken.

This concludes our general analysis of the general properties of the different branch of the large deviation function. In the next section, we consider some special explicit cases.

Additional Explicit Results: Exponential Correlation Function

In the following, we analyse the expression of the large deviation function in the continuous limit for an elastic term of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nu (\textbf{k})=\mu +\textbf{k}^2$$\end{document}ν(k)=μ+k2. The function that we consider in particular is222\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}(\epsilon ,\mu )=\lim _{L\rightarrow \infty }\Lambda _L(e_{L,\mathrm typ}(\mu )+\epsilon ,\mu )\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(ϵ,μ)=limL→∞ΛL(eL,typ(μ)+ϵ,μ).The analysis of the massless limit is done in some detail in section 2.4. In this section, we consider the exponential correlation function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\infty }(q)=\exp (-c q)$$\end{document}f∞(q)=exp(-cq) which corresponds to short-range disorder. The RSB pattern is of 1RSB type in dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<2$$\end{document}d<2, it is marginal for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2 and of FRSB type for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d>2$$\end{document}d>2. In the following we detail the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=1$$\end{document}d=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2$$\end{document}d=2.

In the limit where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}μ→0, the large deviation function only displays an RS branch and reads243\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal{L}(\epsilon )=\lim _{\mu \rightarrow 0}\mathcal{L}_\textrm{RS}(\epsilon +\epsilon _\textrm{RSB}(\mu ),\mu )\end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(ϵ)=limμ→0LRS(ϵ+ϵRSB(μ),μ)244\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad =\frac{c^2}{128\pi ^2}W_{-1}\left( -2e^{\frac{8\pi }{c}\epsilon -2}\right) \left( 2+W_{-1}\left( -2e^{\frac{8\pi }{c}\epsilon -2}\right) \right) \;,\;\;\epsilon \le 0\;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=c2128π2W-1-2e8πcϵ-22+W-1-2e8πcϵ-2,ϵ≤0.Note that in this limit instead, the large deviation function vanishes linearly with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L}'(0)=-c/(4\pi )$$\end{document}L′(0)=-c/(4π).
